Blue boxes represent shareable prompt parts, green boxes\nindicate non-shareable parts and yellow boxes mark non-shareable model outputs. Shareable elements\ninclude few-shot learning examples, questions in self-consistency [53], chat history in multi-turn\nchat, and search history in tree-of-thought [56].\nA\nAdditional Details on RadixAttention\nA.1\nBackground on the KV Cache\nMost LLMs in use today, such as GPT-3 [5], PaLM [9], and LLaMA [49], are based on the autore-\ngressive Transformer architecture [51]. These models predict the probability of the next token in a\nsequence based on the preceding tokens. During inference, the model first processes a sequence of\ninput tokens through a forward pass (this process is called \"prefill\"). It then sequentially decodes\noutput tokens, with each token depending on prior tokens (this process is called \"decoding\"). We\nrefer to the process of taking a sequence of input tokens and generating a sequence of output tokens\nas a single-generation call. Throughout this process, each token generates some intermediate tensors,\nwhich are used for decoding further tokens. These intermediate tensors, known as the \"KV Cache,\"\nare named for the key-value pairs in the self-attention mechanism. An important observation when\ndiscussing optimizations in this paper is that the computation of the KV cache only depends on all\nprevious tokens, so different sequences with the same prefix can reuse the KV cache of the prefix\ntokens and avoid redundant computation.\nOften in LLM programs, multiple text segments and generation calls are appended to a single\nprompt. Caching the computed KV cache for previous tokens across multiple chained calls can\nreduce redundant computation. This optimization, however, is neither free nor trivial, as it requires\nadditional storage and more complex memory management. In addition, it is common in LLM\nprograms to generate multiple outputs from a single prompt or to fork a new prompt from the current\nstate [25]. Basic prefix sharing has been investigated in vLLM [23]. More advanced sharing patterns\nlike irregular tree-structured sharing can also be employed. Fig. 9 shows four typical patterns of KV\ncache sharing across multiple calls; none of the existing systems can automatically handle all of them.\nOn the contrary, our RadixAttention in Sec. 3 can handle all of them automatically at runtime.\nA.2\nPseudocode for Cache-Aware Scheduling\nAlg. 1 shows the pseudocode of cache-aware scheduling for RadixAttention with continuous batching.\nA.3\nProof of the Theorem 3.1\nTheorem 3.1. For a batch of requests, we can achieve an optimal cache hit rate by visiting the radix\ntree of the requests in the depth-first search order, with a cache size ≥the maximum request length.\nThe longest-shared-prefix-first order is equivalent to a depth-first search order.\nProof. First, we show that the depth-first search (DFS) order achieves an optimal cache hit rate. Let\nR denote the set of requests in the batch, and T denote the radix tree built from R. For each edge e\nof T, the KV cache associated with e needs to be computed at least once. Let |e| denote the size of\nthe KV cache associated with e. Let C denote the computational complexity of the KV cache for R.\n14\n\n\n--- PAGE 15 ---\nAlgorithm 1 Cache-Aware Scheduling for RadixAttention with Continuous Batching.\nInput: The radix tree T, the memory pool P, the current running batch B, the waiting queue Q.\nOutput: Finished requests and updated system state.\n// Get all requests from the waiting queue\nrequests ←Q.get_all_requests()\n// Search for the prefix matching for all waiting requests\nfor req ∈requests do\nreq.prefix_node, req.prefix_len ←T.match_prefix(req.input_tokens)\nend for\n// Sort the requests according to the matched prefix lenghts\nrequests.sort()\n// Select requests for the next batch\navailable_size ←T.evictable_size() + P.available_size()\ncurrent_size ←0\nnew_batch ←[]\nfor req ∈requests do\nif req.size() + current_size < available_size then\nnew_batch.append(req)\ndelta ←T.increase_ref_counter(req.prefix_node)\navailable_size ←available_size + delta\nend if\nend for\nQ.remove_requests(new_batch)\n// Insert requests into the current running batch\nB.merge(new_batch)\n// Allocate new memory and do eviction if necessary\nneeded_size ←B.needed_size()\nsuccess, buffer ←P.alloc(needed_size)\nif not success then\nT.evict(needed_size)\nsuccess, buffer ←P.alloc(needed_size)\nend if\nB.run(buffer)\n// Process finished requests\nfinished_requests ←B.drop_finished_requests()\nfor req ∈finished_requests do\nT.decrease_ref_counter(req.prefix_node)\nT.insert(req)\nend for\nreturn finished_requests\nWe obtain the lower bound\nC ≥\nX\ne∈edges(T )\n|e|.\nConsider we visit the radix tree T in DFS order. For each edge e of T, the first time we compute the\nKV cache associated with e, we will then compute the whole subtree of e. During the computation of\nthe subtree of e, the edge e will be continuously hit, so no additional computation will happen. After\nfinishing the computation for the subtree rooted at e, the edge e will not be visited again. Notice that,\nwith a cache size ≥the maximum request length, which equals the longest path in the radix tree T,\nedge e will not be evicted during the computation of its subtree, since the common prefix including e\nof the subtree will be continuously hit. Therefore, the KV cache associated with each edge e will be\ncomputed only once. Thus, we achieve the lower bound\nC =\nX\ne∈edges(T )\n|e|.\n15\n\n\n--- PAGE 16 ---\nThe cache hit rate, defined asP\nr∈R number of cached prefill tokens in r\nP\nr∈R number of prefill tokens in r\n,\nequals 1 −\nC\nP\nr∈R number of prefill tokens, reaches its upper bound, delivering optimality.\nNext, we show that the longest-shared-prefix-first order is equivalent to a DFS order by induction.\n• (Base) In the beginning, since there is nothing cached, a random request that corresponds to\na node x in T will be processed. All requests that correspond to the nodes {v1, ..., vn} on the\npath from the root to x do not need a recomputation. The computation complexity for requests\ncorresponding to the nodes {v1, ..., vn, x} is aligned with a valid DFS. The path from the root to\nx is cached.\n• (Induction) Assume we just visited a node y in T, and the visited nodes align with a DFS order.\nLet P denote the path from the root to y. Then each node that has not been visited has the lowest\ncommon ancestor with the visited nodes on P. Since nodes on P are cached, a node z that has\nnot been visited with the lowest common ancestor on P will have the longest shared prefix. The\nlongest-shared-prefix-first order will select z, which is a valid DFS order. The path from the root\nto z will be cached because it is the most recent.\nIn the online case, the DFS order will be disrupted, but the longest shared prefix schedule still\napproximates the DFS behavior on the augmented part of the implied radix tree. We show this by\nconsidering the step of adding a new batch of requests.\nLet T denote the part of the radix tree that has been visited so far, and T ′ denote the whole new\nradix tree after adding the new batch of requests. Let C denote the set of cached nodes in T. Let\nlongest(C) denote the node in C that has the longest path from the root and has a subtree in T ′ that\nhas not been fully visited.\nThe longest shared prefix schedule will then process the subtree in T ′ rooted at longest(C) in a DFS\norder. During this process, eviction could occur, and the remaining cached nodes from C become\nC(1) ⊆C. A DFS will then occur for the subtree in T ′ rooted at longest(C(1)).\nSimilarly, we will have C(2), . . . , C(k), until C(k) contains only one leaf node in C(k) that has its\nsubtree in T ′ not fully visited. At this point, we have reached a valid DFS state. The remaining part\nof T ′ will be visited in DFS order as described in the proof of Theorem 3.1.\nA.4\nData-Parallel Distributed RadixAttention\nTo adapt the RadixAttention for distributed settings with multiple replica workers (i.e., data par-\nallelism), we developed a mechanism wherein each worker maintains its own sub-tree, while the\nrouter oversees a meta-tree. This meta-tree acts as a trie that tracks all sub-trees and their associated\ndevices. Upon the arrival of a new batch of requests at the router, prefix matching is executed on the\nmeta-tree. We implement various policies based on each request’s affinity—measured by the length\nof the shared prefix with specific workers and other requests in the same group—to make efficient\ndispatch decisions that minimize redundant computations. Each time new requests are processed,\nboth the router and workers independently update their respective trees. Should an eviction occur\nat a worker node, it commits this eviction to a queue, which the router then processes to update the\nmeta-tree during periods of low activity. We benchmarked this distributed configuration using four\nworkers and the MMLU dataset, observing that it achieves linear scaling and an optimal cache hit rate\nwith minimal overhead from this weakly consistent distributed cache design. There exists a trade-off\nbetween maximizing data locality and parallel processing efficiency. Exploring advanced scheduling\npolicies to optimize this trade-off is designated as an area for future research. In addition, concurrent\nwork from Preble [45] studies data-parallel scheduling based on an early version of SGLang.\nB\nAdditional Details on Compressed Finite State Machine\nThis section discusses the background and implementation details of the compressed finite state\nmachine for faster constrained decoding. We aim for LLMs to follow a regular expression (regex),\n16\n\n\n--- PAGE 17 ---\nwhich offers greater expressiveness and can be used to represent common formats such as JSON\nschemas. To achieve this, we convert the regex into a Finite State Machine (FSM) to guide the\ngeneration process during decoding [54]. An FSM is essentially a graph with nodes (states) and\nedges (transitions with strings/characters). Starting from an initial state, each transition appends the\nstring on the edge to move to the next state, using a set of final states to conclude the process. This\nmechanism guides an LLM’s decoding, filtering out invalid tokens based on the FSM’s current state\ntransitions, as illustrated in Fig. 10. The decoding might involve multiple transitions in the FSM\nuntil reaching a final state.\n§ age\n§ Age\n§ hou\n§ 0\n§ 1\n§ ﬁf\n…\nDecode + FSM\nConstrained Decoding With Logits Mask \nDecoding Status\nRegular Expression\n0\n\"\n1\na\n2\ng\n3\ne\n4\n\"\n6\n[0-9]\n5\n:\n[0-9]\n7\n,\nFinite State Machine\nDecoding Status\nDecode + FSM\n✔\n✖\nallowed next token\nnot allowed next token \n✔\n✔\n✖\n✔\n✖\n✖\nFigure 10: Example of how regex is converted into FSM and how FSM guides the decoding process.\nThe challenge of constrained decoding arises from the fact that constraints are often expressed in\nnatural language formats, i.e., regex is depicted by characters/strings, while LLMs are designed to\ninterpret and process these as tokens. This creates a complex scenario since the mapping between\nstrings and tokens is intricate and lacks a one-to-one correspondence [22].\nThis section is derived from an earlier blog post4. Readers are also encouraged to read the blog post\nfor additional background and easier understanding.\nB.1\nImplementation Details of Compressed Finite State Machine\nTo simplify the construction of Compressed FSM, we build the original FSM on characters/strings\ninstead of on tokens. We formally define the concepts of singular transition edge and compressed\nedge as follows:\n• Singular transition edge: A edge is a singular transition edge if 1) its source node has only one\nsuccessor node, and 2) there is only one acceptable character/string on it.\n• Compressed edge: An edge compressed by several consecutively adjacent edges (e0, e1, . . . , ek)\nif and only if e1, . . . , ek are singular transition edges. The text of the compressed edge is the\nconcatenation of the texts of e0, e1, . . . , ek.\nStarting from an FSM based on characters, we recursively merge singular transition edges into\ntheir preceding ones until further compression is unfeasible, resulting in a Compressed FSM. This\napproach speeds up the decoding process, as demonstrated by SGLang’s runtime efficiency with the\nCompressed FSM, shown in Fig. 11.\nB.2\nHandling Tokenization Artifacts with Retokenization\nWhen a new token is generated, we get the token’s string and search all the outgoing edges of the\ncurrent state find the one that starts with the just decoded string, and then move forward. However,\nwhen the transition edge is a well-compressed one and contains a very long string, we may anticipate\nthe next rounds’ decoded strings as well. This is where the acceleration happens and we call this\nprocess Jump Forward. However, we still need to convert the string into tokens for the next decoding\nphases and it is not straightforward due to the LLM’s specific pretraining and tokenization method;\n4https://lmsys.org/blog/2024-02-05-compressed-fsm/\n17\n\n\n--- PAGE 18 ---\nPlease fill in the following \n    information about Harry Potter.\n{\n▁▁▁▁\"name\": ▁\"\nHar\n▁Pot\n\",\nter\n1\nry\n▁▁▁▁ \"age\" :▁\n1\n,\n▁▁▁▁\"house\":▁\"\nG\nryffindor\",\n}\n</s>\nPlease fill in the following \ninformation about Harry Potter.\n{\n\\n\n▁\n▁▁\n▁\"\nname\n\":\nHar\nry\n▁Pot\nter\n\",\n\\n\n▁▁\n▁\n▁\"\nage\n▁\"\n\":\n▁\n1\n1\n,\n\\n\n▁▁\n▁▁\n\"\nh\nous\ne\n\":\n▁\"\nG\nry\nff\nind\nor\n\",\n\\n\n}\n</s>\nJump-Forward Decode With Compressed FSM\nNormal Decode With FSM\nPreﬁll\nDecode\nJump-Forward\nGenerated JSONs\nFigure 11: Comparison of decoding using Compressed FSM versus normal FSM: The left subfigure\ndepicts the decoding process per forward pass, while the right subfigure explains the origins of various\nresult components.\ndirect partitioning might alter the intended meaning [50]. For example, the compressed text in Fig. 2’s\nregex is {\"summary\": \", which can only be tokenized as {\", summary, \": and _\" according to\nthe tokenizer instead of partition them randomly such as {\", summa, ry, and \":_\". To address this\nissue, we use the original tokenizer to retokenize all the previous text as well as the text of the\ncompressed edge, ensuring alignment with the original input format of LLMs. And it only brings\nminor retokenization overhead.\nB.3\nFuture Extension: Addressing Distorted Probability\nThe challenge caused by the gap between strings and tokens also brings the problem of skewed proba-\nbility distribution [50]. For example, in Fig. 2, the regex \"[ABCD][+-]?\" suggests grades from A+ to\nD-, but if replaced with broader terms like Excellent|Above Average|Fair|Below Average,\nthe runtime may inaccurately map an A to Above Average due to the term Above Average is on a\ncompressed transition, misrepresenting the grade hierarchy. This occurs because the LLM doesn’t\nrecognize the specific range of choices, leading to inappropriate token sequences. Computing accurate\nprobabilities for each choice requires summing the probabilities of all token sequences that result\nin each choice, which complicates decoding and adds overhead. One workaround is to include the\nchoices or the regex directly in the prefill prompt, guiding the LLM to be aware of its choices and\noutput the decision in proper token sequences. However, this approach doesn’t solve the underlying\nissue of distorted probabilities, highlighting the need for further research to improve the compressed\nFSM’s accuracy.\nC\nAdditional Experimental Setups and Results\nAdditional experimental setups. Fig. 5 and Fig. 6 are obtained by running Llama-7B on a single\nA10G (24GB) GPU. Fig. 7 are obtained by running Mixtral-8x7B on 8 A10G (24GB) GPUs with\ntensor parallelism. Fig. 8(c) is obtained by running Llama-7B on a single A10G (24GB) GPU. Fig. 12\nare obtained by running Llama-70B on 4 A100G (80GB) GPUs with tensor parallelism. Table 2 are\nobtained by running LLaVA-v1.5-7B on a single A10G (24GB) GPU and running LLaVA-Next-34B\non a single A100G (80GB) GPU. Each bar in the benchmark figures takes several minutes to an hour\nto run.\nAdditional experimental results. Fig. 13 shows the achieved and optimal cache hit rates on the\nbenchmarks listed in Fig. 5. Fig. 12 shows the throughput on Llama-2-70B with tensor parallelism.\nD\nCompiler Mode\nBesides the interpreter mode used in the main body of the paper, another way to run SGLang programs\nis to compile them as computational graphs and execute them with a graph executor. This opens up\n18\n\n\n--- PAGE 19 ---\nMMLU\nReAct\nAgents\nGenerative\nAgents\nTree of\nThought\nSkeleton\nof Thought\nLLM Judge HellaSwag\nJSON\nDecoding\nMulti-Turn\nChat(short)\nMulti-Turn\n Chat(long)\nDSPy RAG\nPipeline\n0.0\n0.2\n0.5\n0.8\n1.0\nThroughput\n(Normalized)\nSGLang\nvLLM\nFigure 12: Normalized throughput on Llama-2-70B models with tensor parallelism. Higher is better.\nMMLU\nReAct\nAgents\nGenerative\nAgents\nTree of\nThought\nSkeleton\nof Thought\nLLM Judge HellaSwag\nJSON\nDecoding\nMulti-Turn\nChat(short)\nMulti-Turn\n Chat(long)\nDSPy RAG\nPipeline\n0.0\n20.0\n40.0\n60.0\n80.0\n100.0\nCache Hit Rate (%)\nAchieved cache hit rate with SGLang\nOptimal cache hit rate\nFigure 13: Achieved cache hit rate and optimal cache hit rate on various benchmarks.\nopportunities for more compilation optimizations, as we can rewrite the graph and perform more\nstatic planning.\nD.1\nDesign and Implementation\nWe designed an intermediate representation (IR) for SGLang, which represents SGLang program\nstructures and operations as a computational graph. This graph includes nodes for primitive operators\nand edges for dependencies. See Fig. 14b for the graph corresponding to the program in Fig. 14a. In\nthe program, each call to a decorated function or fork creates a new prompt state or a stream.\nThere are several types of nodes. Each operand of the operators += and + in a SGLang program\nis represented by an IR node. These include ConstantText, Argument, Gen, Select, Variable,\nFork, GetForkItem, and Join. There are two types of dependencies: intra-stream dependency,\nwhere operations submitted into a stream using += must be executed after all preceding operations\nin that stream, and inter-stream dependency, which occurs when one stream needs to fetch variable\nvalues from another stream, necessitating synchronization. Operations like fork manipulate multiple\nstreams and thus introduce inter-stream dependencies.\nTo generate the graph, we use tracing to run the program with abstract arguments and construct\nthe graph dynamically. This method is limited to programs without data-dependent control flow, a\nlimitation we plan to address in future work. Once constructed, the graph can be executed through\na graph executor, eliminating the need to reinterpret the original Python program. This results in\nbenefits like graph rewriting optimizations, reduced runtime overhead, and program serialization. For\nexecution, stream executors are launched for each data stream, dispatching IR nodes to the streams in\ntopological order.\nD.2\nA Case Study of Compiler Optimization: Code Movement for Improving Prefix Sharing\nWe explore a compilation optimization for SGLang IR: code movement for improving prefix sharing.\nWe anticipate that more classical compiler techniques can also be applied, such as auto-tuning and\ninstruction selection.\nThis optimization aims to improve prefix sharing by reordering nodes in the graph to increase the\nlength of the constant prefix. It does not strictly preserve the original computation, classifying it as\nan aggressive optimization. For instance, changing the prompt from “Here is a question + {question}.\nPlease act as a math expert and solve the given question.” to “Please act as a math expert and solve\nthe given question. Here is a question + {question}.” results in a longer shareable prefix. This\noptimization is interesting because traditional program analysis cannot achieve it due to the presence\nof natural language instructions in SGLang. Instead, we prompt GPT-4 to reorder graph nodes. We\nwrite a prompt with several examples to teach GPT-4 the concepts of SGLang IR, and we find that\nGPT-4 can successfully apply this optimization for some simple SGLang programs.\n19\n\n\n--- PAGE 20 ---\n@function\ndef expand(s, tip):\ns += (\n\"Please expand the following tip into a \"\n\"detailed paragraph: \" + tip + \"\\n\"\n)\ns += gen(\"paragraph\")\n@function\ndef tip_suggestion(s, topic):\ns += \"Here are 2 concise tips for \" + topic + \".\\n”\n# Generate a skeleton of two short tips\ns += \"1.\" + gen(\"tip_1\", stop=[\"\\n\", \":\", \".\"]) + \"\\n\"\ns += \"2.\" + gen(\"tip_2\", stop=[\"\\n\", \":\", \".\"]) + \"\\n”\n# Expand the tips into detailed paragraphs in parallel\ndetailed_tip1 = expand(tip=s[\"tip_1\"])\ndetailed_tip2 = expand(tip=s[\"tip_2\"])\n# Merge the results and generate a summary\ns += \"Tip 1: \" + detailed_tip1[\"paragraph\"] + \"\\n\"\ns += \"Tip 2: \" + detailed_tip2[\"paragraph\"] + \"\\n\"\ns += \"In summary\" + gen(\"summary\")\nstate = tip_suggestion.run(topic=\"staying healthy\")\nprint(state.text())\n(a) The SGLang program for parallel tip suggestion\nwith skeleton-of-thought prompting.\nArgument (topic)\nConstantText (\"Here are …\")\nConstantText (\"1.\")\nConstantText (\"Tip 1: \")\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nGen (\"tip_1\")\nGen (\"tip_2\")\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nConstantText (”2.\")\nConstantText (\"Tip 2: \")\nVariable (\"paragraph\")\nVariable (\"paragraph\")\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nConstantText (\"In summary\")\nGen (name=\"summary\")\nConstantText (\"Please expand …\")\nVariable (\"tip_1\")\nGen(\"paragraph”)\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nConstantText (\"Please expand ..\")\nVariable (\"tip_1\")\nGen(\"paragraph”)\nConstantText (\".\\n\")\nStream 1\nStream 2\nStream 3\n(b) A computational graph for the program in\nFig. 14a. We collect 20 prompt templates from the internet\nand implement them in SGLang. We utilize 5 of these templates as few-shot training examples\nand the remaining 15 as test cases. Our evaluation shows that, for 12 out of the 15 templates,\nGPT-4 successfully reorders the graph nodes without altering the semantics, as confirmed by manual\ninspection of the modified prompts. On average, this optimization results in a 60-token increase in\nthe shareable prefix length, showcasing GPT-4’s effectiveness. Failures in creating optimized prompt\norder come from an incorrect understanding of the semantic meaning behind the graph nodes. It is\ntoo aggressive and puts all constants upfront even when such ordering changes the original semantics.\nThis case study aims to explore the use of GPT-4 for compiler optimizations.
